**[00:07:50]** The first one is church leadership.

**[00:07:52]** Both Orthodox and Catholics have a robust hierarchy

**[00:07:55]** that is far beyond what the Bible prescribes as leadership and how the church is to organize.

**[00:08:01]** Catholics are the worst, obviously, because of the papacy.

**[00:08:04]** But the Orthodox are no different with their patriarchs and metropolitans and everything else.

**[00:08:08]** The New Testament has only three terms that it uses for leaders.

**[00:08:13]** And in fact, out of those three terms,

**[00:08:15]** it's really just two levels of leadership.

**[00:08:17]** There's diakonos, and then the other two are episkopos and presbyteros.

**[00:08:22]** Episkopos and presbyteros are not functionally different.

**[00:08:25]** They're just two words for basically being an elder in the church.

**[00:08:29]** Diakonos refers to people who were in service, who had various gifts,

**[00:08:34]** spiritual gifts or material wealth or various, you know, like strength, being able to help.

**[00:08:39]** They had various talents. They had various connections.

**[00:08:41]** These are people who are ministering to, you know,

**[00:08:45]** leading people in the church.

**[00:08:47]** Whereas presbyteros and episkopos were words that referred to elders.

**[00:08:50]** So I have an important article to read to you about that.

**[00:08:53]** This is a great article that discusses this.

**[00:08:56]** This is from a man named Stephen Armstrong.

**[00:08:59]** And it's written several years ago in 2009, but it discusses these three words.

**[00:09:04]** And it says the following.

**[00:09:05]** There are three terms used in the Greek New Testament to describe the roles of the church leadership.

**[00:09:08]** We can't understand one without also understanding the other two,

**[00:09:11]** since they all work together to provide leadership over Christ's sheep.

**[00:09:14]** What's the difference between a pastor, an elder, an overseer and a deacon?

**[00:09:18]** Episkopos.

**[00:09:20]** This word episkopos, from which we get episkopal,

**[00:09:23]** is used a total of five times in the New Testament,

**[00:09:26]** always in reference to someone who has authority to lead in ministry.

**[00:09:29]** It's usually translated to overseer or bishop.

**[00:09:32]** Clearly, it is a position of leadership,

**[00:09:34]** most commonly thought to refer to the position of pastor.

**[00:09:37]** The difficulty in understanding the full meaning of this word comes when we try to compare it

**[00:09:41]** to the second word used in scripture for leadership,

**[00:09:43]** which is pastor.

**[00:09:44]** Presbyteros.

**[00:09:46]** Presbyteros.

**[00:09:47]** The word presbyteros occurs 72 times in the New Testament,

**[00:09:50]** and it has a range of meanings.

**[00:09:52]** The majority of the time, which is about 57 times,

**[00:09:55]** it is translated as elder,

**[00:09:57]** and means a position of leadership in the church,

**[00:09:59]** like a pastor or other member of the church leadership.

**[00:10:02]** Importantly, it differs from episkopos in that it also assumes the quality of old age.

**[00:10:08]** In other words, elders are leaders in part due to the wisdom and spiritual maturity

**[00:10:12]** they have obtained through a lifelong walk with Christ.

**[00:10:13]** In fact, the word presbyteros is also translated at times to mean older man.

**[00:10:19]** This is about 10 times,

**[00:10:21]** and even as older woman in 1 Timothy 5 verse 2.

**[00:10:25]** But there is still another term for leadership in the Bible we must consider,

**[00:10:29]** diakonos.

**[00:10:30]** The word diakonos means one who serves in ministry,

**[00:10:33]** or more generally, a servant.

**[00:10:35]** The word appears 29 times in the New Testament.

**[00:10:37]** Of those 29 times, it is translated by the NASB as deacon three times,

**[00:10:42]** as minister seven times, and as servant 19 times.

**[00:10:45]** Consequently, the best definition of the duties of a diakonos

**[00:10:48]** is one who ministers to the church through their service.

**[00:10:51]** Considering how Paul uses the term in Philippians 1 verse 1 and 1 Timothy 3,

**[00:10:58]** it is fair to say that he viewed diakonos as playing a leadership role in the church,

**[00:11:02]** albeit a lesser role compared to episkopos and presbyteros.

**[00:11:06]** For example, Paul expected that the determination of who may serve as diakonos

**[00:11:10]** would fall to the other leaders in the church,

**[00:11:13]** clearly suggesting that diakonos answer to the other two positions.

**[00:11:16]** Nevertheless, diakonos are part of the leadership within the church,

**[00:11:19]** and they express their leadership primarily through the acts of service

**[00:11:22]** for the benefit of the entire congregation, including the leaders.

**[00:11:26]** The best example of the appointment of diakonos within the church

**[00:11:29]** is found in Acts 6 where Stephen and six other men are appointed

**[00:11:32]** to positions of service by the primary leaders in that church.

**[00:11:36]** Notice that several of these men eventually became evangelists themselves,

**[00:11:39]** like Stephen.

**[00:11:40]** This is also shown in the book of Acts,

**[00:11:42]** where Stephen and Philip demonstrate that those who begin ministry

**[00:11:45]** in positions of service as diakonos are not precluded

**[00:11:48]** from eventually taking other roles in leadership.

**[00:11:51]** To summarize, then, diakonos are lesser leaders focused on service,

**[00:11:55]** while episkopos and presbyteros are primarily pastoral leaders over a congregation.

**[00:12:01]** Still, several questions remain.

**[00:12:03]** What is the difference between episkopos and presbyteros,

**[00:12:06]** especially in regards to their roles, and what is their relationship to the diakonos?

**[00:12:09]** Three places in Scripture we need to consult for the answers to these questions.

**[00:12:12]** This is a very important understanding, my friends,

**[00:12:15]** because you'll see that there's no functional difference necessarily

**[00:12:19]** between presbyteros and episkopos.

**[00:12:22]** And that is a very key point for church hierarchy,

**[00:12:25]** which shows you that Roman Catholicism and Eastern Orthodoxy

**[00:12:30]** But moving on, the first passage in Acts 20, verse 17 through 28,

**[00:12:34]** in that passage the Apostle Paul is nearing the end of his ministry

**[00:12:37]** and he gives a final word of exhortation to the church in Ephesus.

**[00:12:38]** He begins in verse 17 by addressing the presbyteros, the elders of the church.

**[00:12:44]** Yet a few verses later in that passage, Paul refers to these same men in verse 28

**[00:12:48]** as episkopos, or overseers.

**[00:12:51]** By describing the same group one time as presbyteros and a second time as episkopos,

**[00:12:56]** Paul seems to consider the two words to be synonyms for church leaders.

**[00:13:00]** The second passage of Scripture is Titus 1.

**[00:13:05]** In verse 5, Paul reminds Titus that he left the church,

**[00:13:06]** Paul reminds Titus that he left him in Crete with instructions to appoint presbyteros,

**[00:13:11]** elders, over the city.

**[00:13:13]** In this context, an elder is clearly a position of leadership over the church.

**[00:13:17]** Yet a few verses later, Paul begins to describe the qualifications of an elder

**[00:13:20]** using the word episkopos to describe the same group.

**[00:13:24]** Once again, Paul seems to use the two words for leader interchangeably.

**[00:13:28]** It seems fair to conclude from these two passages that Scripture makes no clear distinction

**[00:13:33]** between presbyteros and episkopos.
